Without the concrete, hands-on jobs and daily routines he had relied on for decades, he began noticing how hard it was to keep track of multi-step plans for gallery walks, lecture series, and his own photography projects, despite carefully written notes and printed maps. As he took on more responsibility coordinating volunteers and communicating with museum staff, he became increasingly frustrated by how often he would start organizing one event and then drift off into reading about art history or rearranging his photo archive, only to realize later that key tasks had been left incomplete. Concerned that his long-standing tendency to \"zone out\" was becoming more disruptive in this new phase of life, and worried it might signal early cognitive decline, he consulted his primary care physician, who then referred him for a psychiatric assessment to clarify whether his persistent difficulties fit an inattentive ADHD pattern rather than a neurocognitive disorder.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since I stopped working full-time, I keep starting projects for the museum and then just never finishing them, even when I really want to.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S002 noticing.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Onset prior to age 12 years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always telling my parents that I was bright but constantly daydreaming, Without the concrete, hands-on jobs and daily routines he had relied on for decades, he began noticing how hard it was to keep track of multi-step plans for gallery walks, lecture series, and his own photography projects, despite carefully written notes and printed maps. As he took on more responsibility coordinating volunteers and communicating with museum staff, he became increasingly frustrated by how often he would start organizing one event and then drift off into reading about art history or rearranging his photo archive, only to realize later that key tasks had been left incomplete. Concerned that his long-standing tendency to \"zone out\" was becoming more disruptive in this new phase of life, and worried it might signal early cognitive decline, he consulted his primary care physician, who then referred him for a psychiatric assessment to clarify whether his persistent difficulties fit an inattentive ADHD pattern rather than a neurocognitive disorder.\n- Chief Complaint: “Since I stopped working full-time, I keep starting projects for the museum and then just never finishing them, even when I really want to.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S002 (Sustained_Attention_Difficulty): When I sit in on those long planning meetings at the museum, I’ll be following along for the first few minutes and then my mind just drifts off to thinking about lighting for a photo I want to take, and by the time I snap back I’ve missed a chunk of what they were talking about.\n- S003 (Listening_Difficulty): My wife and the folks at the gallery will tell me something, like what time we’re meeting or which room we’re using, and I’ll be looking right at them but it’s like the words don’t land, because five minutes later I have to ask them to repeat it.\n- or reading about a new exhibit, and a week later I realize I never finished confirming the room or calling the last few people on the list.\n- S006 (Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance): If I know a task is going to mean sitting still and really concentrating, like writing out a detailed schedule for the month or reading through a stack of museum emails, I keep putting it off and finding little chores to do instead, even though I know it’ll hang over my head until I finally force myself to do it.\n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m always misplacing the things I need for this volunteer work—my reading glasses, the little notebook with all my dates, even the folder with the workshop forms—and I end up walking from room to room retracing my steps because I set them down somewhere without noticing.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Onset prior to age 12 years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always telling my parents that I was bright but constantly daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to hand in homework I’d actually done.\n- functional_impairment: Since retiring, my spacing out and losing track of details has meant I’ve double-booked rooms, missed email deadlines, and had to apologize to museum staff and other volunteers when events weren’t fully organized, which makes me feel unreliable and hesitant to take on new responsibilities.\n- duration: These problems have really come to a head over the last couple of years since I retired and started volunteering at the museum, when the planning and organizing demands Other times, I'll start setting up a workshop and end up sidetracked sorting old photos, only realizing much later that the original task was left completely unfinished."

task is going to mean sitting still and really concentrating, like writing out a detailed schedule for the month or reading through a stack of museum emails, I keep putting it off and finding little chores to do instead, even though I know it’ll hang over my head until I finally force myself to do it.\n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m always misplacing the things I need for this volunteer work—my reading glasses, the little notebook with all my dates, even the folder with the workshop forms—and I end up walking from room to room retracing my steps because I set them down somewhere without noticing.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Onset prior to age 12 years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always telling my parents that I was bright but constantly daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to hand in homework I’d actually done.\n- functional_impairment: Since retiring, my spacing out and losing track of details has meant I’ve double-booked Ever since I retired from my handyman job a couple of years ago, I just can't seem to follow through on these volunteer tasks, and I'm honestly starting to worry it might be a sign of early cognitive decline.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still interested in these museum projects when you start them, but something gets in the way of completing them, or has your interest in activities decreased overall since your retirement?\n\nPatient: Oh, my interest hasn't decreased at all; I'm actually very excited about these projects and really want to do them. The trouble is that I get easily sidetracked by other things, like sorting through my old photos or reading about a new exhibit, and I just struggle to follow through to the end.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're having trouble sitting still or feeling restless, like you need to be constantly doing something, even when you're not working on a project?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel physically restless or have trouble sitting still. It's more that my mind tends to wander and I lose my focus, rather than needing to constantly move around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or hopeless since your retirement, or has your mood been generally stable?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really felt sad, empty, or hopeless. I'm just quite frustrated and worried about my focus and memory since retiring. Aside from that, my mood has been pretty stable.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what happens when you try to focus on one of these museum projects - do you feel like you're intentionally choosing to switch to something else, or does your attention just seem to drift away without you realizing it?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not an intentional choice; my attention just drifts away without me even realizing it. For instance, I’ll be sitting in a planning meeting and suddenly \"snap back\" only to realize I missed a big chunk of what was being discussed. Other times, I'll start setting up a workshop and end up sidetracked sorting old photos, only realizing much later that the original task was left completely unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if someone was working alongside you on these projects, helping to keep you on track, you would be able to complete them, or do you feel like there's something inherent in the way you're approaching tasks that's causing you to not follow through?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, to be honest. I've always tried to handle all of these organizing tasks on my own, so I haven't really thought about whether having someone work alongside me would keep my mind from drifting off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization, time management, or self-regulation of emotions throughout your life, not just since your retirement?\n\nPatient: Yes, these issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, my teachers would tell my parents that I was bright but constantly daydreaming, losing track of instructions, and forgetting to hand in homework I had actually completed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever acted on impulse, doing things without much thought, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or engaging in impulsive behaviors, throughout your life?\n\nPatient: I don't really recall having issues with impulsivity, blurting things out, or interrupting people. My struggles have always been much more about losing focus, daydreaming, and getting sidetracked rather than acting impulsively.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with sustaining attention in tasks or play activities throughout your life, such as making careless mistakes or failing to finish tasks, that are not solely explained by your reported daydreaming?\n\nPatient: Yes, my difficulty with following through on tasks goes way beyond just daydreaming and has been a lifelong issue. Even as a child in school, I would lose track of instructions and forget to hand in homework I had actually completed. Now, that same pattern makes me start setting up museum workshops only to get sidetracked and leave them half-done."

Now, that same pattern makes me start setting up museum workshops only to get sidetracked and leave them half-done.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) or experienced symptoms of inattention, such as difficulty sustaining focus during tasks, that have caused significant impairment in social, occupational, or other areas of functioning throughout your life?\n\nPatient: I have never been formally diagnosed with ADHD, which is why I wanted to get this assessment. I've definitely struggled with inattention my whole life, starting with schoolteachers calling me a daydreamer, and now it's causing real issues with my volunteering, like double-booking rooms and missing deadlines."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
